Actually, looking more carefully at the sequence, the &quot;IHGHYEVAY&quot; motif around position 242-250 is reminiscent of the catalytic His of a Ser-His-Asp triad or Cys-His-Asp triad.

In enzymes with a Cys-His catalytic dyad (like cysteine proteases - papain, cathepsins, etc.):
- His acts as a proton acceptor (base) - it deprotonates the Cys thiol to make it a better nucleophile
- Cys acts as the nucleophile that forms a thioester intermediate (acyl-thioester intermediate) with the substrate

In the papain family:
- Cys is the nucleophile that attacks the substrate&#x27;s carbonyl carbon to form a covalent acyl-enzyme (thioester) intermediate
- His acts as a general base (proton acceptor) that deprotonates the Cys thiol

So the roles would be:
- Cys150: forms acyl-thioester intermediate (nucleophile)
- His245: proton acceptor (general base)

This matches option A: &quot;Cys150: acyl-thioester intermediate; His245: proton acceptor.&quot;

Let me verify this makes biological sense. In cysteine proteases and related enzymes:
1. His acts as a general base, accepting a proton from the Cys thiol (-SH → -S⁻)
2. The deprotonated Cys thiolate attacks the substrate&#x27;s carbonyl carbon
3. This forms a covalent acyl-enzyme intermediate (thioester)
4. Then a water molecule (activated by the now-protonated His) hydrolyzes the thioester

Option A is correct: Cys150 forms the acyl-thioester intermediate (nucleophilic role), and His245 is the proton acceptor (general base role).

Option B reverses the roles, which doesn&#x27;t make chemical sense - His can&#x27;t form an acyl-thioester intermediate (that would require a cysteine or serine nucleophile).

Option C says Cys is only for substrate-binding, which understates its catalytic role.

Option D says His is an NADP-binding residue, which doesn&#x27;t fit the catalytic mechanism.

The answer is A.

2. A region in the middle
3. Sometimes residues from the C-terminal region

The typical binding residues include glycine residues from the Rossmann fold, plus other residues that form hydrogen bonds with the FAD adenine moiety, isoalloxazine ring, and phosphate groups.

Given the options, B seems most likely as it includes all the common binding residues for FAD in this protein family. The inclusion of both Ala103 and Arg120 (in addition to the other residues) provides a comprehensive binding site that would encompass interactions with multiple parts of the FAD molecule.
